They probably think I'm seeing things that aren't there, but I'm not. I'm completely sane, my vision is 20/20, and I don't hear voices or anything crazy like that. \n\nI just... I see what is actually on the screen. Or, well, what *was* on the screen before they scrubbed it. That's the whole problem. I'm seeing the truth, and everyone else is just looking at the sanitized version that someone in medical records cooked up. It's not that I'm seeing things that aren't there, it's that I'm the only one who saw what *was* there before it got deleted. \n\nAnd honestly, it's insulting that they even suggest it. My sister actually asked me that once, she said, 'Are you sure you didn't just imagine it?' Imagine it! I pull these same labs for fifty patients a day! I know what a critical high looks like, I know what a normal range looks like, and I know exactly what I saw on my dad's initial pull. But because the final chart doesn't show it anymore, suddenly I'm the one who's 'seeing things.' It's ridiculous. It's just a convenient way for them to dismiss me without actually having to do the hard work of auditing the system logs. So no, doctor, I don't hear voices and I don't see ghosts. I just see a massive cover-up that no one else has the guts to acknowledge.\n\nDoctor: Outside of this issue with your dad’s lab results, do you generally find it hard to trust other people?\n\nPatient: Trust other people? Well, no, not really. I mean, I wouldn't say I have a general problem with trust, it's not like I'm walking around suspicious of the barista at the coffee shop or thinking my neighbors are up to something. I'm a very rational person. I trust people to do their jobs when they prove they can do them. I trust my coworkers when they follow the proper protocols, I trust the phlebotomists I train because I see them doing the work correctly. \n\nBut when it comes to this... this whole situation with my dad, it's not about a general lack of trust, it's about the fact that they have *broken* my trust. And honestly, how could I trust them? How could anyone trust a system that so blatantly manipulates data and then gaslights the one person who actually caught them doing it? It's not that I'm just inherently distrustful, it's that I've been given every reason to doubt them! \n\nAnd it spills over, you know? It does. Because when you see something like this happen, when you see how easy it is for someone to just go into a system, change a few numbers, erase a few flags, and then have an entire team of doctors and nurses just blindly accept the new version without a single question... it makes you wonder what else they're missing. It makes you wonder how many other patients are sitting there with altered charts, completely unaware. So, if you're asking if I have trust issues, I'd say I have a very healthy, very justified skepticism of a medical establishment that refuses to hold its own people accountable. But outside of that? No, I'm fine. I'm perfectly capable of trusting people who actually deserve it.\n\nDoctor: Aside from your dad’s lab results, do you have any other strong beliefs that other people say aren’t true?\n\nPatient: Other strong beliefs? I mean... no, not really. Because it's not a belief, doctor, it's a fact. I don't go around making up wild theories about things I don't understand. I stick to what I know. And what I know—what I have been saying for a year now, over and over again to anyone who will listen, which, frankly, isn't many people—is that someone in that clinic system is changing or holding back parts of my dad’s lab results. \n\nThat is the only thing I am focused on, because that is the only thing that matters right now. I don't have time to be worried about anything else. I am just so consumed by this, by trying to prove it, because no matter how many doctors look at me with that patronizing smile, or how many family members tell me the numbers are fine, I know the chart they are looking at does not match what I saw. I know it. I saw it with my own eyes on the initial pull before the final push to the EHR. \n\nAnd it's just... it's wearing me down, doctor. It is completely exhausting to be the only person who sees the truth, to be the only person fighting this fight, while everyone else just accepts the sanitized version of the chart. I don't have other \"beliefs\" that people say aren't true, because this isn't a belief. It's a reality that I am trying to expose, and the fact that everyone keeps trying to convince me otherwise is exactly why I'm sitting here right now, just completely worn down by the whole thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel driven to repeatedly check or review the lab records, even when you try not to?\n\nPatient: Driven to check them? Well, I wouldn't use the word \"driven,\" like it's some sort of... compulsion or tick, you know? It's not like I'm sitting there obsessing over washing my hands or checking if the stove is off. It's nothing like that. But do I review the records? Yes, of course I do. I mean, I have to, don't I? \n\nIf I don't check them, who will? I've already established that the doctors are just glancing at the final, altered EHR push and calling it a day. So yes, I log in. I check the portal. I check the timestamps, I look for any discrepancies in the reference ranges, I compare the formatting of the PDFs they generate now versus the ones they generated six months ago, because sometimes they slip up, you know? Sometimes the formatting changes when a record has been manually overridden. I know this because I work with these systems. \n\nAnd it's not that I \"try not to\" check them, it's that I *have* to check them. I'll be sitting at home, trying to watch TV or eat dinner, and I'll just think... what if they changed something else today? What if they updated a note from three months ago to make it look like they caught something they didn't? You can do that, you know, you can add an addendum and backdate the clinical context if you have the right admin privileges. So I pull out my laptop, and I log in, and I go through it line by line. \n\nSometimes I'll be up until 2 or 3 in the morning, just cross-referencing my dad's old printed copies with the current digital ones, looking for the gaps. And it is exhausting. It's so exhausting, doctor. My eyes burn, my back aches from hunching over the screen, and I just want to sleep. I really do. I want to just close the laptop and trust that it's fine. But I can't. Because the minute I stop looking, the minute I let my guard down, that's when they'll bury the original data completely. So no, it's not a compulsion I'm trying to fight. It's a responsibility. I am the only one keeping them honest, even if they won't admit it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think a specific person or group inside the clinic is intentionally altering your dad’s lab results?\n\nPatient: Do I think a specific person or group is intentionally altering them? Yes. Yes, absolutely. I mean, I don't just think it, I know it. I don't know their name, if that's what you're asking. I don't have a specific, you know, John Doe in IT or Dr. Smith in pathology that I can point a finger at, because they hide behind the system. They hide behind the user IDs and the automated audit trails that they probably have the clearance to scrub anyway. But there is absolutely a coordinated effort, or at least a very specific, intentional action being taken by someone with high-level access. \n\nAnd that's why I'm here, doctor. That's what I've been trying to explain for the past year. I've been trying to prove that someone in that clinic system—whether it's a rogue administrator, a covering physician who made a mistake and is covering their tracks, or an IT tech who was told to clean up a file—someone is changing or holding back parts of my dad’s lab results. \n\nI've explained this to the clinic manager, I've explained it to my dad's primary care doctor, I've even tried to explain it to my sister, who just rolled her eyes and told me I was overworking myself. No matter how many doctors or family members tell me the numbers are fine, they are missing the point! They are looking at the *altered* numbers. They are looking at the final, sanitized EHR push. And I know, I *know* for an absolute fact, that the chart they are looking at does not match what I saw on that initial pull. I saw the raw data, doctor. I saw the unflagged values before they were manually overridden to fall within the reference range. \n\nAnd it's just... it's wearing me down. It really is. It's wearing me down to my absolute breaking point. Because how do you fight a ghost in the machine? How do you prove that a file was changed when the people who changed it control the very system that logs the changes? I've been screaming into the void for twelve months, trying to get just one person to look at the discrepancies in the metadata, just one person to acknowledge that the PDF formatting on the hematology panel from October is completely inconsistent with their standard LIS output. But no one will listen. They just pat me on the hand and tell me my dad is fine. But he's not fine if his data is compromised! And I'm not fine having to carry this burden all by myself.\n\nDoctor: For how long have you been certain that someone in the clinic is altering your dad’s lab results?\n\nPatient: Well, like I think I mentioned a few minutes ago, it’s been going on for about a year now. Exactly a year, actually, if you look at the timeline of when his medical problems really started ramping up. \n\nIt was last spring when he first started getting sick, and suddenly we were in and out of that clinic constantly. I mean, it went from him seeing his primary care maybe once a year for a standard physical, to suddenly requiring all these frequent labs and specialist appointments. It was metabolic panels, CBCs, rule-outs... just a constant revolving door of appointments. And you know, being a phlebotomist myself, I'm the one who usually takes him. I'm the one sitting in the chair next to him, watching the techs, knowing exactly what tubes they're drawing—whether it's a lavender top or a tiger top—and I know exactly what assays they're going to run on those samples. \n\nSo when his health issues started and all those appointments started piling up about twelve months ago, that’s when the sheer volume of data started rolling in. And that is exactly when I noticed the shift. That’s when I saw that the initial raw data pulls stopped matching the final, sanitized EHR pushes. Because when you have that many labs being ordered that frequently, it creates a lot of noise, doesn't it? It creates a sort of smokescreen. And whoever is doing this in the clinic, they probably thought, \"Oh, there’s so much data here, the family is overwhelmed, they'll never notice if we tweak a few values to keep him out of the critical tier or hide a mistake.\" \n\nBut they didn't count on me. They didn't count on someone who actually knows how the laboratory information system interfaces with the main electronic health record. They thought I was just some oblivious daughter. So, yes, it's been a year. A whole year of my life, doctor, completely consumed by tracking this, ever since his health issues started and the frequent testing began. And honestly, looking back at how much of my life has been drained by this over the last twelve months... it's just staggering. But I can't stop now, not when I've been tracking their patterns for a whole year.\n\nDoctor: When this first started a year ago, did the belief appear suddenly over a few days?\n\nPatient: Suddenly? Over a few days? I... I really couldn't tell you if it was sudden like that. I don't think I can just put a neat little pin in the calendar and say, \"Oh, it took exactly 48 hours for me to realize what they were doing.\" Because you have to understand what it was actually like back then, doctor. It wasn't just one test. If it was just one test, maybe I would have noticed it instantly. \n\nBut it was a complete whirlwind. We were at the clinic on a Monday for the initial workup, then back on a Wednesday for follow-up draws, then a Friday consult... and every single time, I was the one making sure the orders were right, watching the techs, checking if they were filling the EDTA tubes properly, you know? And then I'd go home, and I'd log into the portal, and the data was just pouring in. It was an absolute flood of numbers. \n\nSo when you ask if the realization happened over a few days... I just don't know. I don't have a clear memory of it being this sudden lightbulb moment, because I was so exhausted, just trying to keep my dad's head above water. I'd be sitting at my kitchen table with all these printouts, comparing them to the screen, and the discrepancies were there, but there was so much noise. So much data. I can't tell you if it hit me all at once over a weekend or what. I really can't. \n\nAll I know is that at some point, the raw data and the final EHR pushes completely stopped aligning, and once I saw it, I couldn't unsee it. But exactly how many days it took to figure that out? I have no idea. I was just too buried in the charts and the portal updates to track my own thoughts like that. It’s not like you just wake up one Tuesday and everything is different, it’s more like... you’re drowning in all these reference ranges and suddenly you realize the life raft they threw you has a hole in it. But I don't know when exactly that clicked.\n\nDoctor: For the past year, has this conviction about your dad’s lab results been present continuously the whole time, or has it only happened during shorter periods?\n\nPatient: Continuous? Or shorter periods? I... I really don't know how to answer that, doctor. I'm not exactly sure what you mean by shorter periods, to be honest. Do you mean, like, do I take a vacation from it? Because you can't exactly take a vacation from the truth, but at the same time... I haven't been sitting around with a stopwatch logging every single minute I spend thinking about his metabolic panels. \n\nI just don't know if it's been one solid, unbroken block of time, or if it comes in waves. I really couldn't tell you. Because, I mean, I have to work, right? I'm at the clinic—my clinic, not his—and I'm drawing blood, I'm labeling vials, I'm scanning barcodes into our own system, and maybe for a few hours, I'm just intensely focused on my own patients. I have to be. You can't miss a vein because you're distracted. So during those hours, am I actively, consciously thinking about whoever is manipulating my dad's chart? I don't know. Maybe not. Maybe it's just running in the background, you know? Like... like a computer program taking up all your memory while you're trying to do something else. \n\nAnd then there are days where we don't have any appointments, and maybe I'm just grocery shopping or doing laundry, and I'm not actively staring at the patient portal. Does that count as a break? Does that mean it's only happening in shorter periods? I honestly have no idea. I haven't kept track of my own headspace like that. I really haven't. I've been way too busy trying to keep track of *their* movements, trying to catch the discrepancies in the data before they get scrubbed. So whether it's been continuous every single second of every single day for twelve months, or if it just spikes every time there's a new lab draw... I just don't have an answer for that. I'm just so tired, doctor. I'm too buried in the numbers to analyze my own thoughts.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.     "content": "Candidate Diseases: \n[\"F22\"]\n\nInterview Transcript: \nDoctor: Hello, it’s good to meet you. My sister actually asked me that once, she said, 'Are you sure you didn't just imagine it?' Imagine it! I pull these same labs for fifty patients a day! Well, no, not really. I trust my coworkers when they follow the proper protocols, I trust the phlebotomists I train because I see them doing the work correctly. \n\nBut when it comes to this... this whole situation with my dad, it's not about a general lack of trust, it's about the fact that they have *broken* my trust. It's not that I'm just inherently distrustful, it's that I've been given every reason to doubt them! \n\nAnd it spills over, you know? It does. It really isn't. I have to keep it all together. So, yes, outside of this one specific nightmare with his data, I am completely functional. I go to work, I pay my bills, I take care of myself. Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nAbsolutely. There is not a single doubt in my mind. I mean, you have to understand, when I say I know what I saw, it’s not like I just glanced at a piece of paper and forgot the numbers. I am in these systems every single day for my job. I know what a raw data pull looks like before it gets finalized in the patient-facing portal. \n\nAnd like I was just saying, for this entire past year, I have been trying to prove this because... well, because it's the truth! Someone in that clinic system is absolutely, without question, changing or holding back parts of his results. And it’s maddening, it really is, because no matter how many times I sit down with his doctors, or no matter how many times my own family members tell me, 'Oh, look at the printout, the numbers are fine, just relax'... I know for a fact the chart they are pointing at does not match what I originally saw on the screen that first day. I know it. \n\nThey look at me like I'm the one who's confused, or like I'm overreacting, but I have the original timestamps memorized. I know the exact reference ranges that were flagged, and now those flags are just... gone. Erased. Like they were never there. So yes, I am completely certain. And honestly, having to fight everyone on this, having to constantly defend what I know is real when they just blindly trust a manipulated chart... it is just wearing me down to nothing. I am so, so tired of being the only one who actually cares enough to see what's right in front of them.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. I’ve been trying to prove that someone in that clinic system is deliberately changing or holding back parts of my dad’s lab results. \n\nAnd people ask me, 'Are you sure? Could you have misread it? Could it be a glitch?' No! I didn't misread it, and it's not a glitch. I do this for a living. I know how the data is entered, I know how the interface looks before the final push to the electronic health record, I know the exact sequence of how a metabolic panel gets verified. But no matter how many doctors I sit across from, or how many of my own family members pat my hand and tell me, 'Oh, the numbers are fine, just look at the printout, you're mistaken'... I know I am not. I am not mistaken. \n\nI know for an absolute fact that the chart they are looking at does not match what I saw on that screen. It just doesn't. The values were different, the flags were different. And having to explain this over and over, having to defend my own eyes and my own professional knowledge while everyone treats me like I'm the one making a mistake... it’s just wearing me down. It’s wearing me down to the point where I can't even focus on anything else because I'm so busy trying to figure out how they manipulated the backend. So no, it is not possible that I am mistaken. Subtypes: Derailment or Loose Associations, Tangentiality, Incoherence or Word Salad, Neologisms, Clang Associations\n[S022] Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.  Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nHear or see things? Like hallucinations? No, no, nothing like that. I mean, I know that's probably what they all think—my family, the doctors, everyone who just looks at me with that pitying expression. They probably think I'm seeing things that aren't there, but I'm not. I'm completely sane, my vision is 20/20, and I don't hear voices or anything crazy like that. \n\nI just... I see what is actually on the screen. Or, well, what *was* on the screen before they scrubbed it. That's the whole problem. I'm seeing the truth, and everyone else is just looking at the sanitized version that someone in medical records cooked up. It's not that I'm seeing things that aren't there, it's that I'm the only one who saw what *was* there before it got deleted. \n\nAnd honestly, it's insulting that they even suggest it. My sister actually asked me that once, she said, 'Are you sure you didn't just imagine it?' Imagine it! I pull these same labs for fifty patients a day! I know what a critical high looks like, I know what a normal range looks like, and I know exactly what I saw on my dad's initial pull. But because the final chart doesn't show it anymore, suddenly I'm the one who's 'seeing things.' It's ridiculous. It's just a convenient way for them to dismiss me without actually having to do the hard work of auditing the system logs. So no, doctor, I don't hear voices and I don't see ghosts. I just see a massive cover-up that no one else has the guts to acknowledge.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt. Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event. It's nothing like that. But do I review the records? Yes, of course I do. I mean, I have to, don't I? \n\nIf I don't check them, who will? I've already established that the doctors are just glancing at the final, altered EHR push and calling it a day. So yes, I log in. I check the portal. I check the timestamps, I look for any discrepancies in the reference ranges, I compare the formatting of the PDFs they generate now versus the ones they generated six months ago, because sometimes they slip up, you know? Sometimes the formatting changes when a record has been manually overridden. I know this because I work with these systems. \n\nAnd it's not that I \"try not to\" check them, it's that I *have* to check them. I'll be sitting at home, trying to watch TV or eat dinner, and I'll just think... what if they changed something else today? What if they updated a note from three months ago to make it look like they caught something they didn't? You can do that, you know, you can add an addendum and backdate the clinical context if you have the right admin privileges. So I pull out my laptop, and I log in, and I go through it line by line. \n\nSometimes I'll be up until 2 or 3 in the morning, just cross-referencing my dad's old printed copies with the current digital ones, looking for the gaps. And it is exhausting. It's so exhausting, doctor. My eyes burn, my back aches from hunching over the screen, and I just want to sleep. I really do. I want to just close the laptop and trust that it's fine. But I can't. Because the minute I stop looking, the minute I let my guard down, that's when they'll bury the original data completely. So no, it's not a compulsion I'm trying to fight. It's a responsibility. I am the only one keeping them honest, even if they won't admit it.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }
]

Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. I don't have a specific, you know, John Doe in IT or Dr. Smith in pathology that I can point a finger at, because they hide behind the system. They hide behind the user IDs and the automated audit trails that they probably have the clearance to scrub anyway. That's what I've been trying to explain for the past year. I've been trying to prove that someone in that clinic system—whether it's a rogue administrator, a covering physician who made a mistake and is covering their tracks, or an IT tech who was told to clean up a file—someone is changing or holding back parts of my dad’s lab results. \n\nI've explained this to the clinic manager, I've explained it to my dad's primary care doctor, I've even tried to explain it to my sister, who just rolled her eyes and told me I was overworking myself. No matter how many doctors or family members tell me the numbers are fine, they are missing the point! They are looking at the *altered* numbers. They are looking at the final, sanitized EHR push. And I know, I *know* for an absolute fact, that the chart they are looking at does not match what I saw on that initial pull. I saw the raw data, doctor. I saw the unflagged values before they were manually overridden to fall within the reference range. \n\nAnd it's just... it's wearing me down. It really is. It's wearing me down to my absolute breaking point. Because how do you fight a ghost in the machine? How do you prove that a file was changed when the people who changed it control the very system that logs the changes? I've been screaming into the void for twelve months, trying to get just one person to look at the discrepancies in the metadata, just one person to acknowledge that the PDF formatting on the hematology panel from October is completely inconsistent with their standard LIS output. Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions). Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected. I'm the one sitting in the chair next to him, watching the techs, knowing exactly what tubes they're drawing—whether it's a lavender top or a tiger top—and I know exactly what assays they're going to run on those samples. \n\nSo when his health issues started and all those appointments started piling up about twelve months ago, that’s when the sheer volume of data started rolling in. And that is exactly when I noticed the shift. That’s when I saw that the initial raw data pulls stopped matching the final, sanitized EHR pushes. Because when you have that many labs being ordered that frequently, it creates a lot of noise, doesn't it? It creates a sort of smokescreen. And whoever is doing this in the clinic, they probably thought, \"Oh, there’s so much data here, the family is overwhelmed, they'll never notice if we tweak a few values to keep him out of the critical tier or hide a mistake.\" \n\nBut they didn't count on me. They didn't count on someone who actually knows how the laboratory information system interfaces with the main electronic health record. They thought I was just some oblivious daughter. So, yes, it's been a year. A whole year of my life, doctor, completely consumed by tracking this, ever since his health issues started and the frequent testing began. We were at the clinic on a Monday for the initial workup, then back on a Wednesday for follow-up draws, then a Friday consult... and every single time, I was the one making sure the orders were right, watching the techs, checking if they were filling the EDTA tubes properly, you know? And then I'd go home, and I'd log into the portal, and the data was just pouring in. It was an absolute flood of numbers. \n\nSo when you ask if the realization happened over a few days... I just don't know. I don't have a clear memory of it being this sudden lightbulb moment, because I was so exhausted, just trying to keep my dad's head above water. I'd be sitting at my kitchen table with all these printouts, comparing them to the screen, and the discrepancies were there, but there was so much noise. So much data. I can't tell you if it hit me all at once over a weekend or what. I really can't. \n\nAll I know is that at some point, the raw data and the final EHR pushes completely stopped aligning, and once I saw it, I couldn't unsee it. Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.
